					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">John Quincy Adams</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 131 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-131" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<p><b>John Quincy Adams</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="&#39;w&#39; in &#39;wind&#39;">w</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="&#39;z&#39; in &#39;zoom&#39;">z</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/d\/dd\/En-John_Quincy_Adams.oga&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;En-John Quincy Adams.oga&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dd/En-John_Quincy_Adams.oga" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:En-John_Quincy_Adams.oga" title="File:En-John Quincy Adams.oga">i</a></sup></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> July 11, 1767 – February 23, 1848) was an American statesman, politician, diplomat, lawyer, and <a href="/wiki/Diarist" class="mw-redirect" title="Diarist">diarist</a> who served as the sixth <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a>, from 1825 to 1829. He previously served as the eighth <a href="/wiki/United_States_secretary_of_state" class="mw-redirect" title="United States secretary of state">United States secretary of state</a> from 1817 to 1825. During his long diplomatic and political career, Adams served as an ambassador and also as a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">United States Congress</a> representing <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts" title="Massachusetts">Massachusetts</a> in both chambers. He was the eldest son of <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a>, who served as the second president of the United States from 1797 to 1801, and <a href="/wiki/First_Lady_of_the_United_States" title="First Lady of the United States">First Lady</a> <a href="/wiki/Abigail_Adams" title="Abigail Adams">Abigail Adams</a>. Initially a <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist</a> like his father, he won election to the presidency as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a>, and later, in the mid-1830s, became affiliated with the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>.
</p><p>Born in <a href="/wiki/Braintree,_Massachusetts" title="Braintree, Massachusetts">Braintree, Massachusetts</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Adams spent much of his youth in Europe, where his father served as a diplomat. After returning to the United States, Adams established a successful legal practice in <a href="/wiki/Boston" title="Boston">Boston</a>. In 1794, President <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> appointed Adams as the U.S. ambassador to the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Netherlands</a>, and Adams would serve in high-ranking diplomatic posts until 1801, when <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> took office as president. Federalist leaders in Massachusetts arranged for Adams's election to the United States Senate in 1802, but Adams broke with the Federalist Party over foreign policy and was denied re-election. In 1809, President <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a>, a member of the Democratic-Republican Party, appointed Adams as the U.S. ambassador to <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>. <a href="/wiki/List_of_multilingual_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="List of multilingual presidents of the United States">Multilingual</a>, Adams held diplomatic posts for the duration of Madison's presidency, and he served as part of the American delegation that negotiated an end to the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>. In 1817, President <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a> selected Adams as his secretary of state. In that role, Adams negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a>, which provided for the American acquisition of Florida. He also helped formulate the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>, which became a key tenet of <a href="/wiki/U.S._foreign_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. foreign policy">U.S. foreign policy</a>. In 1818, Adams was elected a member of the <a href="/wiki/American_Philosophical_Society" title="American Philosophical Society">American Philosophical Society</a> in Philadelphia.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William H. Crawford</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>—all members of the Democratic-Republican Party—competed in the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 presidential election</a>. Because no candidate won a majority of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>, the House of Representatives held a <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election#1825_contingent_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">contingent election</a>, which Adams won with the support of <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the United States House of Representatives">Speaker of the House</a> Henry Clay, whom Adams would controversially appoint as his secretary of state. As president, Adams called for an ambitious agenda that included <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">federally funded infrastructure projects</a>, the establishment of a national university, and engagement with the countries of Latin America, but Congress refused to pass many of his initiatives. During Adams's presidency, the Democratic-Republican Party split into two major camps: the <a href="/wiki/National_Republican_Party" title="National Republican Party">National Republican Party</a>, which supported Adams, and Andrew Jackson's <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a>. The Democrats proved to be more effective political organizers than Adams and his National Republican supporters, and Jackson soundly defeated Adams in the <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 presidential election</a>, making Adams the second president to fail to win re-election (his father being the first).
</p><p>Rather than retiring from public service, Adams won election to the House of Representatives, where he would serve from 1831 until his death in 1848. He remains the only former president to be elected to the chamber. After narrowly losing his bids for <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Massachusetts" title="Governor of Massachusetts">Governor of Massachusetts</a> and Senate re-election, Adams joined the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a> in the early 1830s before joining the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>, which united those opposed to President Jackson. During his time in Congress, Adams became increasingly critical of <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slavery</a> and of the Southern leaders whom he believed controlled the Democratic Party. He was particularly opposed to the <a href="/wiki/Texas_annexation" title="Texas annexation">annexation of Texas</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a>, which he saw as a war to extend slavery and <a href="/wiki/Slave_Power" title="Slave Power">its political grip on Congress</a>. He also led the repeal of the "<a href="/wiki/Gag_rule_(United_States)" title="Gag rule (United States)">gag rule</a>", which had prevented the House of Representatives from debating petitions to <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolish slavery</a>. Historians concur that Adams was one of the greatest diplomats and secretaries of state in American history; they <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">typically rank</a> him as an average president, as he had an ambitious agenda but could not get it passed by Congress. By contrast, historians also view Adams in a more positive light during his post-presidency because of his vehement stance against slavery, as well as his fight for the rights of women and Native Americans.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life.2C_education.2C_and_early_career"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life,_education,_and_early_career">Early life, education, and early career</span></h2>
<p>John Quincy Adams was born on July 11, 1767, to <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abigail_Adams" title="Abigail Adams">Abigail Adams (née Smith)</a> in a part of <a href="/wiki/Braintree,_Massachusetts" title="Braintree, Massachusetts">Braintree</a>, <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Massachusetts" class="mw-redirect" title="Province of Massachusetts">Massachusetts</a>, that is now <a href="/wiki/Quincy,_Massachusetts" title="Quincy, Massachusetts">Quincy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He was named after his mother's maternal grandfather, Colonel <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy" title="John Quincy">John Quincy</a>, after whom Quincy, Massachusetts, is also named. Colonel Quincy died two days after his great-grandson's birth.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Young Adams was educated by private tutors – his cousin James Thaxter and his father's law clerk, Nathan Rice.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2018)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He soon exhibited literary skills, and in 1779 he started a diary that he kept until just before he died in 1848.<sup id="cite_ref-Masshist.org_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Masshist.org-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Until the age of ten, Adams grew up on the family farm in Braintree, largely in the care of his mother. Though frequently absent because of his participation in the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>, John Adams maintained a correspondence with his son, encouraging him to read works by authors such as <a href="/wiki/Thucydides" title="Thucydides">Thucydides</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hugo_Grotius" title="Hugo Grotius">Hugo Grotius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> With his father's encouragement, Adams would also translate <a href="/wiki/Classical_antiquity" title="Classical antiquity">classical</a> authors such as <a href="/wiki/Virgil" title="Virgil">Virgil</a>, <a href="/wiki/Horace" title="Horace">Horace</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plutarch" title="Plutarch">Plutarch</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1778, Adams and his father departed for Europe, where John Adams would serve as part of American diplomatic missions in <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_France" title="Kingdom of France">France</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Netherlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> During this period, Adams studied <a href="/wiki/Law" title="Law">law</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>, and attended several schools, including <a href="/wiki/Leiden_University" title="Leiden University">Leiden University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In 1781, Adams traveled to <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Saint Petersburg</a>, Russia, where he served as the secretary to the American diplomat, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Dana" title="Francis Dana">Francis Dana</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He returned to the Netherlands in 1783 and accompanied his father to <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain" title="Kingdom of Great Britain">Great Britain</a> in 1784.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Though Adams enjoyed Europe, he and his family decided he needed to return to the United States to complete his education and eventually launch a political career.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams returned to the United States in 1785 and earned admission as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Junior_(education_year)" title="Junior (education year)">junior</a> class of <a href="/wiki/Harvard_College" title="Harvard College">Harvard College</a> the following year. He joined <a href="/wiki/Phi_Beta_Kappa" title="Phi Beta Kappa">Phi Beta Kappa</a> and excelled academically, graduating second in his class in 1787.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> After graduating from Harvard, he <a href="/wiki/Reading_law" title="Reading law">studied law</a> with <a href="/wiki/Theophilus_Parsons" title="Theophilus Parsons">Theophilus Parsons</a> in <a href="/wiki/Newburyport,_Massachusetts" title="Newburyport, Massachusetts">Newburyport, Massachusetts</a>, from 1787 to 1789.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Adams initially opposed the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">United States Constitution</a>, but he ultimately came to accept the document, and in <a href="/wiki/1789_United_States_presidential_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1789 United States presidential election">1789</a> his father was elected as the first <a href="/wiki/Vice_president_of_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Vice president of the United States">vice president of the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 1790, Adams opened his own legal practice in <a href="/wiki/Boston" title="Boston">Boston</a>. Despite some early struggles, he was successful as an attorney and established financial independence from his parents.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_political_career_.281793.E2.80.931817.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career_(1793–1817)">Early political career (1793–1817)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Electoral_history_of_John_Quincy_Adams" title="Electoral history of John Quincy Adams">Electoral history of John Quincy Adams</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_diplomatic_career_and_marriage">Early diplomatic career and marriage</span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti <p>Adams initially avoided becoming involved in politics, instead focusing on building his legal career. In 1791, he wrote a series of pseudonymously published essays arguing that Britain provided a better governmental model than France. Two years later, he published another series of essays attacking <a href="/wiki/Edmond-Charles_Gen%C3%AAt" title="Edmond-Charles Genêt">Edmond-Charles Genêt</a>, a French diplomat who sought to undermine President <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>'s policy of neutrality in the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolutionary_Wars" title="French Revolutionary Wars">French Revolutionary Wars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In 1794, Washington appointed Adams as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_Netherlands" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to the Netherlands">U.S. ambassador to the Netherlands</a>. Adams considered declining the role, but ultimately took the position on the advice of his father.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> While abroad, Adams continued to urge neutrality, arguing that the United States would benefit economically by staying out of the ongoing French Revolutionary Wars.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> His chief duty as the ambassador to the Netherlands was to secure and maintain loans essential to U.S. finances. On his way to the Netherlands, he met with <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a>, who was then negotiating the <a href="/wiki/Jay_Treaty" title="Jay Treaty">Jay Treaty</a> with Great Britain. Adams supported the Jay Treaty, but it proved unpopular with many in the United States, contributing to a growing partisan split between the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist Party</a> of <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a> of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In 1794 he supported <a href="/wiki/John_Skey_Eustace" title="John Skey Eustace">John Skey Eustace</a> who wanted to return to the United States via the Netherlands.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Adams spent the winter of 1795–1796 in <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>, where he met <a href="/wiki/Louisa_Adams" title="Louisa Adams">Louisa Catherine Johnson</a>, the second daughter of American merchant Joshua Johnson. In April 1796, Louisa accepted Adams's proposal of marriage. Adams's parents disapproved of his decision to marry a woman who had grown up in England, but he informed his parents that he would not reconsider his decision.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Adams initially wanted to delay his wedding to Louisa until he returned to the United States, but they married in <a href="/wiki/All_Hallows-by-the-Tower" title="All Hallows-by-the-Tower">All Hallows-by-the-Tower</a> on July 26, 1797.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after the wedding, Joshua Johnson fled England to escape his creditors, and Adams did not receive the dowry that Johnson had promised him, much to the embarrassment of Louisa. Adams noted in his own diary that he had no regrets about his decision to marry Louisa.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1796, Washington appointed Adams as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Portugal" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Portugal">U.S. ambassador to Portugal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, John Adams defeated Jefferson in the <a href="/wiki/1796_United_States_presidential_election" title="1796 United States presidential election">1796 presidential election</a>. When the elder Adams became president, he appointed his son as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Germany">U.S. ambassador to Prussia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Though concerned that his appointment would be criticized as <a href="/wiki/Nepotism" title="Nepotism">nepotistic</a>, Adams accepted the position and traveled to the Prussian capital of <a href="/wiki/Berlin" title="Berlin">Berlin</a> with his wife and his younger brother, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Boylston_Adams_(1772%E2%80%931832)" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Boylston Adams (1772–1832)">Thomas Boylston Adams</a>. The State Department tasked Adams with developing commercial relations with Prussia and Sweden, but President Adams also asked his son to write to him frequently about affairs in Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In 1799, Adams negotiated a <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Amity_and_Commerce_(Prussia%E2%80%93United_States)" title="Treaty of Amity and Commerce (Prussia–United States)">new trade agreement between the United States and Prussia</a>, though he could never complete an agreement with Sweden.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> He frequently wrote to family members in the United States, and in 1801 his letters about the Prussian region of <a href="/wiki/Silesia" title="Silesia">Silesia</a> were published in a book titled <i>Letters on Silesia</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> During his time in Prussia, Adams befriended the German diplomat and writer <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_von_Gentz" title="Friedrich von Gentz">Friedrich von Gentz</a>, whose work, <i>The Origins and Principles of the American Revolution, Compared with the Origins and Principles of the French Revolution,</i> Adams would translate into English in 1800. In the <a href="/wiki/1800_United_States_presidential_election" title="1800 United States presidential election">1800 presidential election</a>, Jefferson defeated John Adams, and both Adams and his son left office in early 1801.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="U.S._senator_from_Massachusetts">U.S. senator from Massachusetts</span></h3>
<p>On his return to the United States, Adams re-established a legal practice in <a href="/wiki/Boston" title="Boston">Boston</a>, and in April 1802 he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts_Senate" title="Massachusetts Senate">Massachusetts Senate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In November of that year, he ran unsuccessfully for the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">United States House of Representatives</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In February 1803, the Massachusetts legislature elected Adams to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a>. Though somewhat reluctant to affiliate with any political party, Adams joined the Federalist minority in Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Like his Federalist colleagues, he opposed the impeachment of Associate Justice <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Chase" title="Samuel Chase">Samuel Chase</a>, an outspoken supporter of the Federalist Party.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams had strongly opposed Jefferson's 1800 presidential candidacy, but he gradually became alienated from the Federalist Party. His disaffection was driven by the party's declining popularity, disagreements over foreign policy, and Adams's hostility to <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Pickering" title="Timothy Pickering">Timothy Pickering</a>, a Federalist Party leader whom Adams viewed as overly favorable to Britain. Unlike other New England Federalists, Adams supported the Jefferson administration's <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a> and expansionist policies.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Adams was the lone Federalist in Congress to vote for the <a href="/wiki/Non-importation_Act" title="Non-importation Act">Non-importation Act</a> of 1806 that punished Britain for its attacks on American shipping during the ongoing <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>. Adams became increasingly frustrated with the unwillingness of other Federalists to condemn British actions, including <a href="/wiki/Impressment" title="Impressment">impressment</a>, and he moved closer to the Jefferson administration. After Adams supported the <a href="/wiki/Embargo_Act_of_1807" title="Embargo Act of 1807">Embargo Act of 1807</a>, the Federalist-controlled Massachusetts legislature elected Adams's successor several months before the end of his term, and Adams resigned from the Senate shortly thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While a member of the Senate, Adams served as a professor of logic at <a href="/wiki/Brown_University" title="Brown University">Brown University</a><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and as the <a href="/wiki/Boylston_Professorship_of_Rhetoric_and_Oratory" title="Boylston Professorship of Rhetoric and Oratory">Boylston Professor of Rhetoric and Oratory</a> at <a href="/wiki/Harvard_University" title="Harvard University">Harvard University</a>. Adams's devotion to classical rhetoric shaped his response to public issues, and he would remain inspired by those rhetorical ideals long after the neo-classicalism and deferential politics of the founding generation were eclipsed by the commercial ethos and mass democracy of the <a href="/wiki/Jacksonian_democracy" title="Jacksonian democracy">Jacksonian Era</a>. Many of Adams's idiosyncratic positions were rooted in his abiding devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Cicero" title="Cicero">Ciceronian</a> ideal of the citizen-orator "speaking well" to promote the welfare of the polis.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He was also influenced by the classical republican ideal of civic eloquence espoused by British philosopher <a href="/wiki/David_Hume" title="David Hume">David Hume</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Adams adapted these classical republican ideals of public oratory to the American debate, viewing its multilevel political structure as ripe for "the renaissance of Demosthenic eloquence". His <i>Lectures on Rhetoric and Oratory</i> (1810) looks at the fate of ancient oratory, the necessity of liberty for it to flourish, and its importance as a unifying element for a new nation of diverse cultures and beliefs. Just as civic eloquence failed to gain popularity in Britain, in the United States interest faded in the second decade of the 19th century, as the "public spheres of heated oratory" disappeared in favor of the private sphere.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_to_Russia">Minister to Russia</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg/180px-1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg/270px-1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg/360px-1815_US_passport_-_LONDON.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3201" data-file-height="4647" /></a><figcaption>1815 US passport issued by John Quincy Adams at London.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After resigning from the Senate, Adams was ostracized by Massachusetts Federalist leaders, but he declined Democratic-Republican entreaties to seek office.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In 1809, he argued before the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court of the United States</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Fletcher_v._Peck" title="Fletcher v. Peck">Fletcher v. Peck</a></i>, and the Supreme Court ultimately agreed with Adams's argument that the Constitution's <a href="/wiki/Contract_Clause" title="Contract Clause">Contract Clause</a> prevented the state of Georgia from invalidating a land sale to out-of-state companies.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, President <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a> appointed Adams as the first <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Russia">United States Minister to Russia</a> in 1809. Though Adams had only recently broken with the Federalist Party, his support of Jefferson's foreign policy had earned him goodwill with the Madison Administration.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Adams was well-qualified for the role after his experiences in Europe generally and Russia specifically.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After a difficult passage through the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Sea" title="Baltic Sea">Baltic Sea</a>, Adams arrived in the Russian capital of St. Petersburg in October 1809. He quickly established a productive working relationship with Russian official <a href="/wiki/Nikolay_Rumyantsev" title="Nikolay Rumyantsev">Nikolay Rumyantsev</a> and eventually befriended Tsar <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia" title="Alexander I of Russia">Alexander I of Russia</a>. Adams continued to favor American neutrality between France and Britain during the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Napoleonic War">Napoleonic War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Louisa was initially distraught at the prospect of living in Russia, but she became a popular figure at the Russian court.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> From his diplomatic post, Adams observed the French Emperor <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a>'s <a href="/wiki/French_invasion_of_Russia" title="French invasion of Russia">invasion of Russia</a>, which ended in defeat for the French.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> In February 1811, President Madison nominated Adams as an <a href="/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_United_States_Supreme_Court" class="mw-redirect" title="Associate Justice of the United States Supreme Court">Associate Justice of the United States Supreme Court</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The nomination was unanimously confirmed by the Senate, but Adams declined the seat, preferring a career in politics and diplomacy, so <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Story" title="Joseph Story">Joseph Story</a> took the seat instead.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Treaty_of_Ghent_and_ambassador_to_Britain">Treaty of Ghent and ambassador to Britain</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-Gilbert_Stuart_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3883" data-file-height="4697" /></a><figcaption>Adams portrait – <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Stuart" title="Gilbert Stuart">Gilbert Stuart</a>, 1818</figcaption></figure>
<p>Adams had long feared that the United States would enter a war it could not win against Britain, and by early 1812, he saw such a war as inevitable due to the constant British attacks on American shipping and the British practice of <a href="/wiki/Impressment" title="Impressment">impressment</a>. In mid-1812, the United States declared war against Britain, beginning the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>. Tsar Alexander attempted to mediate the conflict between Britain and the United States, and President Madison appointed Adams, Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/Albert_Gallatin" title="Albert Gallatin">Albert Gallatin</a>, and Federalist Senator <a href="/wiki/James_A._Bayard_(politician,_born_1767)" title="James A. Bayard (politician, born 1767)">James A. Bayard</a> to a delegation charged with negotiating an end to the war. Gallatin and Bayard arrived in St. Petersburg in July 1813, but the British declined Tsar Alexander's offer of mediation. Hoping to start negotiations at another venue, Adams left Russia in April 1814.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Negotiations finally began in mid-1814 in <a href="/wiki/Ghent" title="Ghent">Ghent</a>, where Adams, Gallatin, and Bayard were joined by two additional American delegates, <a href="/wiki/Jonathan_Russell" title="Jonathan Russell">Jonathan Russell</a> and former <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the United States House of Representatives">Speaker of the House</a> <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Adams, the nominal head of the delegation, got along well with Gallatin, Bayard, and Russell, but he occasionally clashed with Clay.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British delegation initially treated the United States as a defeated power, demanding the creation of an <a href="/wiki/Indian_barrier_state" title="Indian barrier state">Indian barrier state</a> from American territory near the <a href="/wiki/Great_Lakes" title="Great Lakes">Great Lakes</a>. The American delegation unanimously rejected this offer, and their negotiating position was bolstered by the American victory in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Plattsburgh" title="Battle of Plattsburgh">Battle of Plattsburgh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> By November 1814, the government of <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a> decided to seek an end to hostilities with the U.S. on the basis of <i>status quo ante bellum</i>. Even though a return to the status quo would mean the continuation of the British practice of impressment, Adams and his fellow commissioners had hoped for similar terms. The treaty was signed on December 24, 1814. The United States did not gain any concessions from the treaty but could boast that it had survived a war against the strongest power in the world. Following the signing of the treaty, Adams traveled to Paris, where he witnessed first-hand the <a href="/wiki/Hundred_Days" title="Hundred Days">Hundred Days</a> of Napoleon's restoration.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1815, Adams learned that President Madison had appointed him as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to the United Kingdom">U.S. ambassador to Britain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> With the aid of Clay and Gallatin, Adams negotiated a limited trade agreement with Britain. Following the conclusion of the trade agreement, much of Adams's time as ambassador was spent helping stranded American sailors and prisoners of war.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=307–11,_318–20_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=307–11,_318–20-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> In pursuit of national unity, newly elected president <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a> decided a Northerner would be optimal for the position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a>, and he chose the respected and experienced Adams for the role.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Having spent several years in Europe, Adams returned to the United States in August 1817.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=307–11,_318–20_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=307–11,_318–20-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_.281817.E2.80.931825.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_(1817–1825)">Secretary of State (1817–1825)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Monroe" title="Presidency of James Monroe">Presidency of James Monroe</a></div>
<p>Adams served as Secretary of State during Monroe's eight-year presidency, from 1817 to 1825. Many of his successes as secretary, such as the convention of 1818 with the United Kingdom, the Transcontinental Treaty with Spain, and the Monroe Doctrine, were not preplanned strategies but responses to unexpected events. Adams wanted to delay American recognition of the newly independent republics of Latin America to avoid the risk of war with Spain and its European allies. However, Andrew Jackson's military campaign in Florida and Henry Clay's threats in Congress forced Spain to cut a deal, which Adams negotiated successfully. Biographer James Lewis says, "He managed to play the cards that he had been dealt – cards that he very clearly had not wanted – in ways that forced the Spanish cabinet to recognize the weakness of its own hand".<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Apart from the Monroe doctrine, his last four years as Secretary of State were less successful because he was preoccupied with his presidential campaign and refused to make compromises with other countries that might have weakened his candidacy; the result was a small-scale trade war but a successful election to the White House.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png/220px-John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png/330px-John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png/440px-John_quincy_adams_-_thomas_sully.png 2x" data-file-width="5504" data-file-height="6688" /></a><figcaption>Painting of John Quincy Adams by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Sully" title="Thomas Sully">Thomas Sully</a>, 1824</figcaption></figure>
<p>Taking office in the aftermath of the War of 1812, Adams thought that the country had been fortunate in avoiding territorial losses, and he prioritized avoiding another war with a European power, particularly Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=321–22_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=321–22-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> He also sought to avoid exacerbating sectional tensions, which had been a major issue for the country during the War of 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> One of the major challenges confronting Adams was how to respond to the power vacuum in Latin America that arose from Spain's weakness following the <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_War" title="Peninsular War">Peninsular War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> In addition to his foreign policy role, Adams held several domestic duties, including overseeing the 1820 <a href="/wiki/United_States_Census" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Census">Census</a> and writing an extensive report on <a href="/wiki/Weights_and_measures" class="mw-redirect" title="Weights and measures">weights and measures</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The weights and measures report, a particular passion of Adams', provided an extensive historical perspective on the topic and advocated for adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Metric_system" title="Metric system">metric system</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe and Adams agreed on most major foreign policy issues: both favored neutrality in <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_wars_of_independence" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin American wars of independence">Latin American independence wars</a>, peace with the United Kingdom, rejection of a trade agreement with the French, and peaceful expansion into the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a>'s North American territories.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The president and his secretary of state developed a strong working relationship, and while Adams often influenced Monroe's policies, he respected that Monroe made the final decisions on major issues.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Monroe met regularly with his five-person <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_States" title="Cabinet of the United States">cabinet</a>, which initially consisted of Adams, Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William H. Crawford</a>, Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a>, Secretary of the Navy <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Williams_Crowninshield" title="Benjamin Williams Crowninshield">Benjamin Crowninshield</a>, and Attorney General <a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Adams developed a strong respect for Calhoun but believed that Crawford was unduly focused on succeeding Monroe in 1824.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as ambassador to Britain, Adams had begun negotiations over several contentious issues that had not been solved by the War of 1812 or the Treaty of Ghent. In 1817, the two countries agreed to the <a href="/wiki/Rush%E2%80%93Bagot_Treaty" title="Rush–Bagot Treaty">Rush–Bagot Treaty</a>, which limited naval armaments on the <a href="/wiki/Great_Lakes" title="Great Lakes">Great Lakes</a>. Negotiations between the two powers continued, resulting in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_1818" title="Treaty of 1818">Treaty of 1818</a>, which defined the <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_border" title="Canada–United States border">Canada–United States border</a> west of the Great Lakes. The boundary was set at the <a href="/wiki/49th_parallel_north" title="49th parallel north">49th parallel</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Rocky_Mountains" title="Rocky Mountains">Rocky Mountains</a>, while the territory to the west of the mountains, known as <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a>, would be jointly occupied. The agreement marked a watershed moment in <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="United Kingdom–United States relations">United Kingdom–United States</a> relations, as the United States focused on its southern and western borders and British concerns about American expansionism subsided.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Adams.E2.80.93On.C3.ADs_Treaty"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Adams–Onís_Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Adams_onis_map.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/330px-Adams_onis_map.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="270" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/495px-Adams_onis_map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/660px-Adams_onis_map.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="654" /></a><figcaption>In the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a>, the United States acquired Florida and set the western border of the 1803 <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Adams took office, Spanish possessions bordered the United States to the south and west. To the south, Spain retained control of <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Florida" title="Spanish Florida">Florida</a>, which the U.S. had long sought to purchase. Spain struggled to control the Indian tribes active in Florida, and some of those tribes raided United States territory. To the west, <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">New Spain</a> bordered the territory acquired by the United States in the Louisiana Purchase, but no clear boundary had been established between United States and Spanish territory.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=321–22_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=321–22-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> After taking office, Adams began negotiations with <a href="/wiki/Luis_de_On%C3%ADs" title="Luis de Onís">Luis de Onís</a>, the Spanish minister to the United States, for the purchase of Florida and the settlement of a border between the United States and New Spain. The negotiations were interrupted by an escalation of the <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars" title="Seminole Wars">Seminole War</a>, and in December 1818, Monroe ordered General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> to enter Florida and retaliate against <a href="/wiki/Seminole" title="Seminole">Seminoles</a> that had raided Georgia. Exceeding his orders, Jackson captured the Spanish outposts of <a href="/wiki/St._Marks,_Florida" title="St. Marks, Florida">St. Marks</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pensacola,_Florida" title="Pensacola, Florida">Pensacola</a> and executed two Englishmen. While Jackson's actions outraged the rest of the cabinet, Adams defended them as necessary to the country's self-defense, and he eventually convinced Monroe and most of the cabinet to support Jackson.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=333–337,_348_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=333–337,_348-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Adams informed Spain that its failure to police its own territory had compelled Jackson to act, and he advised Spain to either secure the region or sell it to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The British, meanwhile, declined to risk their recent rapprochement with the United States, and did not make a major diplomatic issue out of Jackson's execution of two British nationals.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Negotiations between Spain and the United States continued, and Spain agreed to cede Florida. The determination of the western boundary of the United States proved more difficult. American expansionists favored setting the border at the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Grande" title="Rio Grande">Rio Grande</a>, but Spain, intent on protecting its colony of <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">Mexico</a> from American encroachment, insisted on setting the boundary at the <a href="/wiki/Sabine_River_(Texas%E2%80%93Louisiana)" title="Sabine River (Texas–Louisiana)">Sabine River</a>. At Monroe's direction, Adams agreed to the <a href="/wiki/Sabine_River_(Texas%E2%80%93Louisiana)" title="Sabine River (Texas–Louisiana)">Sabine River</a> boundary, but he insisted that Spain cede its claims on Oregon Country.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Adams was deeply interested in establishing American control over the Oregon Country, partly because he believed that control of that region would spur trade with Asia. The acquisition of Spanish claims to the Pacific Northwest also allowed the Monroe administration to pair the acquisition of Florida, which was chiefly sought by Southerners, with territorial gains favored primarily by those in the North.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> After extended negotiations, Spain and the United States agreed to the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a>, which was ratified in February 1821.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=333–337,_348_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=333–337,_348-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Adams was deeply proud of the treaty, though he privately was concerned by the potential expansion of slavery into the newly acquired territories.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In 1824, the Monroe administration would strengthen US claims to Oregon by ratifying the <a href="/wiki/Russo-American_Treaty_of_1824" title="Russo-American Treaty of 1824">Russo-American Treaty of 1824</a>, which established <a href="/wiki/Russian_America" title="Russian America">Russian Alaska</a>'s southern border at 54°40′ north.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Monroe_Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a></div>
<p>As the Spanish Empire continued to fracture during Monroe's second term, Adams, Monroe and Clay became increasingly concerned that the "<a href="/wiki/Holy_Alliance" title="Holy Alliance">Holy Alliance</a>" of <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a>, Austria, and Russia would seek to bring Spain's erstwhile colonies under their control, to the point of even contemplating a Holy Alliance of their own to defend democracy. In his 1821 Fourth of July address, Adams addressed this issue, noting a shared "chain of sympathy" between the U.S. and Latin America, but arguing for neutrality rather than a Holy Alliance.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> In 1822, following the conclusion of the Adams–Onís Treaty, the Monroe administration recognized the independence of several Latin American countries, including Argentina and Mexico. In 1823, British Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/George_Canning" title="George Canning">George Canning</a> suggested that the United States and Britain should work together to preserve the independence of these fledgling republics. The cabinet debated whether to accept the offer, but Adams opposed it. Instead, Adams urged Monroe to publicly declare the United States' opposition to any European attempt to colonize or re-take control of territory in the Americas, while also committing the United States to neutrality in European affairs. In his December 1823 <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_Union" title="State of the Union">annual message to Congress</a>, Monroe laid out the Monroe Doctrine, which was largely built upon Adams's ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-kaplan380385_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaplan380385-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In issuing the Monroe Doctrine, the United States displayed a new level of assertiveness in international relations, as the doctrine represented the country's first claim to a <a href="/wiki/Sphere_of_influence" title="Sphere of influence">sphere of influence</a>. It also marked the country's shift in psychological orientation away from Europe and towards the Americas. Debates over foreign policy would no longer center on relations with Britain and France, but instead focus on western expansion and relations with Native Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> The doctrine became one of the foundational principles of <a href="/wiki/History_of_U.S._foreign_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="History of U.S. foreign policy">U.S. foreign policy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kaplan380385_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaplan380385-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="1824_presidential_election">1824 presidential election</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1824.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/ElectoralCollege1824.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1824.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/ElectoralCollege1824.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1824.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/ElectoralCollege1824.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1824.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="577" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1824 presidential election results</figcaption></figure>
<p>Immediately upon becoming Secretary of State, Adams emerged as one of Monroe's most likely successors, as the last three presidents had all served in the role before taking office. As the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 election</a> approached, Henry Clay, John C. Calhoun (who later dropped out of the race), and William H. Crawford appeared to be Adams's primary competition to succeed Monroe.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Crawford favored state sovereignty and a <a href="/wiki/Strict_constructionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Strict constructionist">strict constructionist</a> view of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">Constitution</a>, while Clay, Calhoun, and Adams embraced federally funded <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a>, high <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariffs</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a>, which was also known as the national bank.<sup id="cite_ref-Howe_2007_203–204_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howe_2007_203–204-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Because the Federalist Party had all but collapsed after the War of 1812, all the major presidential candidates were members of the Democratic-Republican Party.<sup id="cite_ref-parsons7072_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-parsons7072-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Adams felt that his own election as president would vindicate his father, while also allowing him to pursue an ambitious domestic policy. Though he lacked the charisma of his competitors, Adams was widely respected and benefited from the lack of other prominent Northern political leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams's top choice for the role of vice president was General Andrew Jackson; Adams noted that "the Vice-Presidency was a station in which [Jackson] could hang no one, and in which he would need to quarrel with no one".<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> However, as the 1824 election approached, Jackson jumped into the race for president, and Calhoun ended up receiving the Vice-presidential support of Adams supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-parsons7072_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-parsons7072-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> While the other candidates based their candidacies on their long tenure as congressmen, ambassadors, or members of the cabinet, Jackson's appeal rested on his military service, especially in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Congressional_nominating_caucus" title="Congressional nominating caucus">congressional nominating caucus</a> had decided upon previous Democratic-Republican presidential nominees, but it had become largely discredited by 1824. Candidates were instead nominated by state legislatures or nominating conventions, and Adams received the endorsement of the New England legislatures.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> The regional strength of each candidate played an important role in the election; Adams was popular in <a href="/wiki/New_England" title="New England">New England</a>, Clay and Jackson were strong in the West, and Jackson and Crawford competed for the South.<sup id="cite_ref-kaplan386389_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaplan386389-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<table class="wikitable" style="margin:0 0 1em 1em; float:right;">

</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In the 1824 presidential election, Jackson won a plurality in the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">Electoral College</a>, taking 99 of the 261 electoral votes, while Adams won 84, Crawford won 41, and Clay took 37. Calhoun, meanwhile, won a majority of the electoral votes for vice president.<sup id="cite_ref-kaplan386389_95-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaplan386389-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Adams nearly swept the electoral votes of New England and won a majority of the electoral votes in <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a>, but he won just six electoral votes from the slave states. Most of Jackson's support came from slave-holding states, but he also won <a href="/wiki/New_Jersey" title="New Jersey">New Jersey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a>, and some electoral votes from the Northwest.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> As no candidate won a majority of the electoral votes, the House was required to hold a contingent election under the terms of the <a href="/wiki/Twelfth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twelfth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twelfth Amendment</a>. The House would decide among the top three electoral vote winners, with each state's delegation having one vote; thus, unlike his three rivals, Clay was not eligible to be elected by the House.<sup id="cite_ref-kaplan386389_95-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaplan386389-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams knew that his own victory in the contingent election would require the support of Clay, who wielded immense influence in the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Though they were quite different in temperament and had clashed in the past, Adams and Clay shared similar views on national issues. By contrast, Clay viewed Jackson as a dangerous demagogue, and he was unwilling to support Crawford due to the latter's health issues.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Adams and Clay met before the contingent election, and Clay agreed to support Adams in the election.<sup id="cite_ref-kap391393_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kap391393-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Adams also met with Federalists such as <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a>, promising that he would not deny governmental positions to members of their party.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> On February 9, 1825, Adams won the contingent election on the first ballot, taking 13 of the 24 state delegations. Adams won the House delegations of all the states in which he or Clay had won a majority of the electoral votes, as well as the delegations of <a href="/wiki/Illinois" title="Illinois">Illinois</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louisiana" title="Louisiana">Louisiana</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Maryland" title="Maryland">Maryland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kap391393_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kap391393-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Adams's victory made him the first child of a president to serve as president himself.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> After the election, many of Jackson's supporters claimed that Adams and Clay had reached a "<a href="/wiki/Corrupt_Bargain" class="mw-redirect" title="Corrupt Bargain">Corrupt Bargain</a>" whereby Adams promised Clay the position of Secretary of State in return for Clay's support.<sup id="cite_ref-kap391393_99-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kap391393-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281825.E2.80.931829.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1825–1829)">Presidency (1825–1829)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_John_Quincy_Adams" title="Presidency of John Quincy Adams">Presidency of John Quincy Adams</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration">Inauguration</span></h3>
<p>Adams was <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_of_John_Quincy_Adams" title="Inauguration of John Quincy Adams">inaugurated</a> on March 4, 1825, becoming the first son of a former United States president to himself become president, a feat only repeated 176 years later by <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a>, son of <a href="/wiki/George_H._W._Bush" title="George H. W. Bush">George H. W. Bush</a>. As Adams took the <a href="/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_president_of_the_United_States#Use_of_Bibles" title="Oath of office of the president of the United States">oath of office</a>, he departed from tradition by placing his hand on a book of <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_law" title="Constitutional law">constitutional law</a> instead of on a <a href="/wiki/Bible" title="Bible">Bible</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In his inaugural address, he adopted a post-partisan tone, promising to avoid party-building and politically motivated appointments. He also proposed an elaborate program of "internal improvements": roads, ports, and canals. Though some were worried about the constitutionality of such federal projects, Adams argued that the <a href="/wiki/Taxing_and_Spending_Clause" title="Taxing and Spending Clause">General Welfare Clause</a> provided for broad constitutional authority. He promised that he would ask Congress to authorize many such projects.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administration">Administration</span></h3>
<table class="infobox" style="width:auto;text-align:left;line-height:1.2em;margin-left:1em; margin-right:0; float:right; clear:right;"><tbody><tr><th colspan="3" style="line-height:1.5em;font-size:110%;background:#DCDCDC;text-align:center">The Adams cabinet</th></tr><tr><th>Office</th><th>Name</th><th>Term</th></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#000"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold">John Quincy Adams</th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">Vice President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Richard_Rush" title="Richard Rush">Richard Rush</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/James_Barbour" title="James Barbour">James Barbour</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1828</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Peter_Buell_Porter" title="Peter Buell Porter">Peter Buell Porter</a></th><td>1828&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Attorney_General" title="United States Attorney General">Attorney General</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Samuel_L._Southard" title="Samuel L. Southard">Samuel L. Southard</a></th><td>1825&#8211;1829</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Adams presided over a harmonious and productive cabinet that he met with on a weekly basis.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Like Monroe, Adams sought a geographically balanced cabinet that would represent the various party factions, and he asked the members of the Monroe cabinet to remain in place for his own administration.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Samuel_L._Southard" title="Samuel L. Southard">Samuel L. Southard</a> of <a href="/wiki/New_Jersey" title="New Jersey">New Jersey</a> stayed on as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a> kept his post of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Attorney_General" title="United States Attorney General">Attorney General</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/John_McLean" title="John McLean">John McLean</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ohio" title="Ohio">Ohio</a> continued to serve as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General" title="United States Postmaster General">Postmaster General</a>, an important position that was not part of the cabinet at that time.<sup id="cite_ref-Parsons_2009_pages=106–107_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parsons_2009_pages=106–107-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Adams's first choices for <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a> and <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a> were Andrew Jackson and William Crawford, but each declined to serve in the administration. Adams instead selected <a href="/wiki/James_Barbour" title="James Barbour">James Barbour</a> of <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a>, a prominent supporter of Crawford, to lead the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_War" title="United States Department of War">War Department</a>. Leadership of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Department of the Treasury">Treasury Department</a> went to <a href="/wiki/Richard_Rush" title="Richard Rush">Richard Rush</a> of <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a>, who would become a prominent advocate of internal improvements and protective tariffs within the administration.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Adams chose <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a>, angering those who believed Clay had offered Adams his support in the 1824 election in exchange for the most prestigious position in the cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Clay would later regret accepting the job since it reinforced the "Corrupt Bargain" accusation. However, Clay's strength in the West and interest in foreign policy made him a natural choice for the position.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_affairs">Domestic affairs</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ambitious_agenda">Ambitious agenda</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg/220px-Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="216" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg/330px-Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg/440px-Medal_of_John_Quincy_Adams_MET_LC-83_2_429-001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2523" data-file-height="2480" /></a><figcaption>Medal of John Quincy Adams</figcaption></figure>
<p>In his 1825 annual message to Congress,<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Adams presented a comprehensive and ambitious agenda. He called for major investments in internal improvements as well as the creation of a national university, a naval academy, and a national astronomical observatory. Noting the healthy status of the treasury and the possibility for more revenue via land sales, Adams argued for the completion of several projects that were in various stages of construction or planning, including a road from <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a> to <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> He also proposed the establishment of a Department of the Interior as a new cabinet-level department that would preside over these internal improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Adams hoped to fund these measures primarily through Western land sales, rather than increased taxes or public debt.<sup id="cite_ref-Howe_2007_203–204_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howe_2007_203–204-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> The domestic agenda of Adams and Clay, which would come to be known as the <a href="/wiki/American_System_(economic_plan)" title="American System (economic plan)">American System</a>, was designed to unite disparate regional interests in the promotion of a thriving national economy.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams's programs faced opposition from various quarters. Many disagreed with his broad interpretation of the constitution and preferred that power be concentrated in state governments rather than the federal government. Others disliked interference from any level of government and were opposed to central planning.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=404–405_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=404–405-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Some in the South feared that Adams was secretly an <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolitionist</a> and that he sought to subordinate the states to the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Most of the president's proposals were defeated in Congress. Adams's ideas for a national university, a national observatory, and the establishment of a uniform system of weights and measures never received congressional votes.<sup id="cite_ref-remini8486_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-remini8486-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> His proposal for the creation of a naval academy won the approval of the Senate but was defeated in the House; opponents objected to the naval academy's cost and worried that the establishment of such an institution would "produce degeneracy and corruption of the public morality".<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Adams's proposal to establish a <a href="/wiki/History_of_bankruptcy_law_in_the_United_States" title="History of bankruptcy law in the United States">national bankruptcy law</a> was also defeated.<sup id="cite_ref-remini8486_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-remini8486-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unlike other aspects of his domestic agenda, Adams won congressional approval for several ambitious infrastructure projects.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Between 1824 and 1828, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army_Corps_of_Engineers" title="United States Army Corps of Engineers">United States Army Corps of Engineers</a> conducted surveys for a bevy of potential roads, canals, railroads, and improvements in river navigation.
