Adams presided over major repairs and further construction on the National Road, and shortly after he left office the National Road extended from <a href="/wiki/Cumberland,_Maryland" title="Cumberland, Maryland">Cumberland, Maryland</a>, to <a href="/wiki/Zanesville,_Ohio" title="Zanesville, Ohio">Zanesville, Ohio</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> The Adams administration also saw the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake_and_Ohio_Canal" title="Chesapeake and Ohio Canal">Chesapeake and Ohio Canal</a>; the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake_%26_Delaware_Canal" title="Chesapeake &amp; Delaware Canal">Chesapeake &amp; Delaware Canal</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Louisville_and_Portland_Canal" title="Louisville and Portland Canal">Louisville and Portland Canal</a> around the <a href="/wiki/Falls_of_the_Ohio" class="mw-redirect" title="Falls of the Ohio">Falls of the Ohio</a>; the connection of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Lakes" title="Great Lakes">Great Lakes</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_River" title="Ohio River">Ohio River system</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ohio" title="Ohio">Ohio</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indiana" title="Indiana">Indiana</a>; and the enlargement and rebuilding of the <a href="/wiki/Dismal_Swamp_Canal" title="Dismal Swamp Canal">Dismal Swamp Canal</a> in <a href="/wiki/North_Carolina" title="North Carolina">North Carolina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, the first passenger railroad in the United States, the <a href="/wiki/Baltimore_and_Ohio_Railroad" title="Baltimore and Ohio Railroad">Baltimore and Ohio Railroad</a>, was constructed during Adams's presidency. Though many of these projects were undertaken by private actors, the government often provided money or land to aid the completion of such projects.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Formation_of_political_parties">Formation of political parties</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/First_Party_System" title="First Party System">First Party System</a> and <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/180px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/270px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/360px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="610" data-file-height="739" /></a><figcaption>General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>, Adams's opponent in the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824</a> and <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 United States presidential elections</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the immediate aftermath of the 1825 contingent election, Jackson was gracious to Adams.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Adams's appointment of Clay rankled Jackson, who received a flood of letters encouraging him to run. In 1825, Jackson accepted the presidential nomination of the <a href="/wiki/Tennessee" title="Tennessee">Tennessee</a> legislature for the 1828 election.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Though he had been close to Adams during Monroe's presidency, Vice President Calhoun was also politically alienated from the president by the appointment of Clay, since that appointment established Clay as the natural heir to Adams.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Adams's ambitious December 1825 annual message to Congress further galvanized the opposition, with important figures such as <a href="/wiki/Francis_Preston_Blair" title="Francis Preston Blair">Francis Preston Blair</a> of <a href="/wiki/Kentucky" title="Kentucky">Kentucky</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hart_Benton_(politician)" title="Thomas Hart Benton (politician)">Thomas Hart Benton</a> of <a href="/wiki/Missouri" title="Missouri">Missouri</a> breaking with the Adams administration.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> By the end of the first session of the <a href="/wiki/19th_United_States_Congress" title="19th United States Congress">19th United States Congress</a>, an anti-Adams congressional coalition consisting of Jacksonians (led by Benton and <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Lawson_White" title="Hugh Lawson White">Hugh Lawson White</a>), Crawfordites (led by <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nathaniel_Macon" title="Nathaniel Macon">Nathaniel Macon</a>), and Calhounites (led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Y._Hayne" title="Robert Y. Hayne">Robert Y. Hayne</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_McDuffie" title="George McDuffie">George McDuffie</a>) had emerged.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Aside from Clay, Adams lacked strong supporters outside of the North, and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Everett" title="Edward Everett">Edward Everett</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_W._Taylor_(politician)" title="John W. Taylor (politician)">John Taylor</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a> served as his strongest advocates in Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-Parsons_2009_pages=138–139_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parsons_2009_pages=138–139-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Supporters of Adams began calling themselves <a href="/wiki/National_Republicans" class="mw-redirect" title="National Republicans">National Republicans</a>, while supporters of Jackson began calling themselves <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democrats</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> In the press, they were often described as "Adams Men" and "Jackson Men".<sup id="cite_ref-Howe_2007_251_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howe_2007_251-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/United_States_elections,_1826" class="mw-redirect" title="United States elections, 1826">1826 elections</a>, Adams's opponents picked up seats throughout the country, as allies of Adams failed to coordinate among themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-Parsons_2009_pages=125–126_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parsons_2009_pages=125–126-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Stevenson" title="Andrew Stevenson">Andrew Stevenson</a>, a Jackson supporter, replaced John Taylor, an Adams supporter, as Speaker of the House.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> As Adams himself noted, the United States had never seen a Congress that was firmly under the control of political opponents of the president.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> After the elections, Van Buren and Calhoun agreed to throw their support behind Jackson in 1828, with Van Buren bringing along many of Crawford's supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Though Jackson did not articulate a detailed political platform in the same way that Adams did, his coalition united in opposition to Adams's reliance on government planning.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Adams, meanwhile, clung to the hope of a non-partisan nation, and he refused to make full use of the power of patronage to build up his own party structure.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tariff_of_1828">Tariff of 1828</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg/220px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg/330px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg/440px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_Charles_Osgood.jpg 2x" data-file-width="743" data-file-height="919" /></a><figcaption>Painting of Quincy Adams by Charles Osgood, 1828</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the first half of his administration, Adams avoided taking a strong stand on tariffs, partly because he wanted to avoid alienating his allies in the South and New England.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> After Jacksonians took power in 1827, they devised a tariff bill designed to appeal to Western states while instituting high rates on imported materials important to the economy of New England. It is unclear whether Van Buren, who shepherded the bill through Congress, meant for the bill to pass, or if he had deliberately designed it to force Adams and his allies to oppose it.<sup id="cite_ref-abominations1_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abominations1-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Regardless, Adams signed the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1828" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariff of 1828">Tariff of 1828</a>, which became known as the "Tariff of Abominations" by opponents. Adams was denounced in the South, and he received little credit for the tariff in the North.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Indian_policy">Indian policy</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Federal_Indian_Policy" title="Federal Indian Policy">Federal Indian Policy</a></div>
<p>Adams sought the gradual assimilation of <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Native Americans</a> via consensual agreements, a priority shared by few whites in the 1820s. Yet Adams was also deeply committed to the westward expansion of the United States. Settlers on the frontier, constantly seeking to move westward, cried for a more expansionist policy that disregarded the concerns of Native Americans. Early in his term, Adams suspended the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Indian_Springs_(1825)" title="Treaty of Indian Springs (1825)">Treaty of Indian Springs</a> after learning that the Governor of Georgia, <a href="/wiki/George_Troup" title="George Troup">George Troup</a>, had forced the treaty on the <a href="/wiki/Muscogee" title="Muscogee">Muscogee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Adams signed a new treaty with the Muscogee in January 1826 that allowed the Muscogee to stay but ceded most of their land to Georgia. Troup refused to accept its terms and authorized all Georgian citizens to evict the Muscogee.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> A showdown between Georgia and the federal government was only averted after the Muscogee agreed to a third treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Though many saw Troup as unreasonable in his dealings with the federal government and the Native Americans, the administration's handling of the incident alienated those in the <a href="/wiki/Deep_South" title="Deep South">Deep South</a> who favored immediate <a href="/wiki/Indian_removal" title="Indian removal">Indian removal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h3>
<p>Adams famously said "America goes not abroad, in search of monsters to destroy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdel2014300_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdel2014300-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Trade_and_claims">Trade and claims</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_Clay.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Henry_Clay.JPG/180px-Henry_Clay.JPG" decoding="async" width="180" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Henry_Clay.JPG/270px-Henry_Clay.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Henry_Clay.JPG/360px-Henry_Clay.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2898" data-file-height="3496" /></a><figcaption>Quincy Adams appointed <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> as Secretary of State</figcaption></figure>
<p>One of the major foreign policy goals of the Adams administration was the expansion of American trade.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> His administration reached <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(international_relations)" title="Reciprocity (international relations)">reciprocity</a> treaties with a number of nations, including Denmark, <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Central_America" title="Federal Republic of Central America">Federal Republic of Central America</a>. The administration also reached commercial agreements with the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hawaii" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Hawaii">Kingdom of Hawaii</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tahiti" title="Kingdom of Tahiti">Kingdom of Tahiti</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Agreements with Denmark and Sweden opened their colonies to American trade, but Adams was especially focused on opening trade with the <a href="/wiki/British_West_Indies" title="British West Indies">British West Indies</a>. The United States had reached a commercial agreement with Britain in 1815, but that agreement excluded British possessions in the Western Hemisphere. In response to United States pressure, the British had begun to allow a limited amount of American imports to the West Indies in 1823, but United States leaders continued to seek an end to Britain's protective <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Preference" title="Imperial Preference">Imperial Preference</a> system.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> In 1825, Britain banned United States trade with the <a href="/wiki/British_West_Indies" title="British West Indies">British West Indies</a>, dealing a blow to Adams's prestige.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=400–401_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=400–401-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> The Adams administration negotiated extensively with the British to lift this ban, but the two sides could not reach an agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Despite the loss of trade with the British West Indies, the other commercial agreements secured by Adams helped expand the overall volume of United States exports.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Latin_America">Latin America</span></h4>
<p>Aside from an unsuccessful attempt to purchase <a href="/wiki/Mexican_Texas" title="Mexican Texas">Texas</a> from Mexico, President Adams did not seek to expand into <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a> or North America.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Adams and Clay instead sought engagement with Latin America to prevent it from falling under the British Empire's economic influence.<sup id="cite_ref-Howe_2007_257_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howe_2007_257-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> As part of this goal, the administration favored sending a United States delegation to the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Panama" title="Congress of Panama">Congress of Panama</a>, an 1826 conference of New World republics organized by <a href="/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar" title="Simón Bolívar">Simón Bolívar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=401–402_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=401–402-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Clay and Adams hoped that the conference would inaugurate a "<a href="/wiki/Good_Neighbor_policy" title="Good Neighbor policy">Good Neighborhood Policy</a>" among the independent states of the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-Remini_2002_pages=82–83_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Remini_2002_pages=82–83-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> However, the funding for a delegation and the confirmation of delegation nominees became entangled in a political battle over Adams's domestic policies, with opponents such as Van Buren impeding the confirmation of a delegation.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_2014_pages=404–405_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kaplan_2014_pages=404–405-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> While Van Buren saw the Panama Congress as an unwelcome deviation from the more <a href="/wiki/United_States_non-interventionism" title="United States non-interventionism">isolationist</a> foreign policy established by President Washington,<sup id="cite_ref-Remini_2002_pages=82–83_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Remini_2002_pages=82–83-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> many Southerners opposed involvement with any conference attended by delegates from <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a>, a republic that had been established through a <a href="/wiki/Haitian_Revolution" title="Haitian Revolution">slave revolt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Though the United States delegation finally won confirmation from the Senate, it never reached the Congress of Panama due to the Senate's delay.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1828_presidential_election">1828 presidential election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1828.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/ElectoralCollege1828.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1828.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/ElectoralCollege1828.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1828.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/ElectoralCollege1828.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1828.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="577" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1828 presidential election results</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Jacksonians formed an effective party apparatus that adopted many modern campaign techniques. Rather than focusing on issues, they emphasized Jackson's popularity and the supposed corruption of Adams and the federal government. Jackson himself described the campaign as a "struggle between the virtue of the people and executive patronage".<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Adams, meanwhile, refused to adapt to the new reality of political campaigns, and he avoided public functions and refused to invest in pro-administration tools such as newspapers.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> In early 1827, Jackson was publicly accused of having encouraged his wife, <a href="/wiki/Rachel_Jackson" title="Rachel Jackson">Rachel</a>, to desert her first husband.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In response, followers of Jackson attacked Adams's personal life, and the campaign turned increasingly nasty.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> The Jacksonian press portrayed Adams as an out-of-touch elitist,<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> while pro-Adams newspapers attacked Jackson's past involvement in various duels and scuffles, portraying him as too emotional and impetuous for the presidency. Though Adams and Clay had hoped that the campaign would focus on the American System, it was instead dominated by the personalities of Jackson and Adams.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Vice President Calhoun joined Jackson's ticket, while Adams turned to Secretary of the Treasury Richard Rush as his running mate.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The 1828 election thus marked the first time in United States history that a presidential ticket composed of two Northerners faced off against a presidential ticket composed of two Southerners.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> In the election, Jackson won 178 of the 261 electoral votes and just under 56% of the popular vote.<sup id="cite_ref-parsons181183_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-parsons181183-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Jackson won 50.3% of the popular vote in the free states, but 72.6% of the vote in the slave states.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> No future presidential candidate would match Jackson's proportion of the popular vote until <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a>'s 1904 campaign, while Adams's loss made him the second one-term president, after his own father.<sup id="cite_ref-parsons181183_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-parsons181183-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> By 1828, only two states did not hold a popular vote for president, and the number of votes in the 1828 election was triple that in the 1824 election. This increase in votes was due not only to the recent wave of democratization, but also because of increased interest in elections and the growing ability of the parties to mobilize voters.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Adams did not attend Jackson's inauguration, making him one of only four presidents who finished their terms but skipped the event.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_congressional_career_.281830.E2.80.931848.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_congressional_career_(1830–1848)">Later congressional career (1830–1848)</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Jackson_administration.2C_1830.E2.80.931836"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Jackson_administration,_1830–1836">Jackson administration, 1830–1836</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Wrinkled, elderly man with spectacles" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg/220px-John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="305" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg/330px-John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg/440px-John_Quincy_Adams_-_copy_of_1843_Philip_Haas_Daguerreotype.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3083" data-file-height="4270" /></a><figcaption>Daguerreotype of Quincy Adams by Philip Haas, 1843</figcaption></figure>
<p>Adams considered permanently retiring from public life after his 1828 defeat, and he was deeply hurt by the suicide of his son, <a href="/wiki/George_Washington_Adams" title="George Washington Adams">George Washington Adams</a>, in 1829.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> He was appalled by many of the Jackson administration's actions, including its embrace of the <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils system</a><sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> and the prosecution of his close friend, Treasury Auditor <a href="/wiki/Tobias_Watkins" title="Tobias Watkins">Tobias Watkins</a>, for embezzlement.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Though they had once maintained a cordial relationship, Adams and Jackson each came to loathe the other in the decades after the 1828 election.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Adams grew bored with his retirement and still felt that his career was unfinished, so he ran for and won a seat in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">United States House of Representatives</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections,_1830" class="mw-redirect" title="United States House of Representatives elections, 1830">1830 elections</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> His election went against the generally held opinion, shared by his own wife and youngest son, that former presidents should not run for public office.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, he would win election to nine terms, serving from 1831 until his death in 1848.<sup id="cite_ref-bioguide1_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bioguide1-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Adams and <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a> are the only <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States#Activities" title="President of the United States">former presidents to serve in Congress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-millerleg_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-millerleg-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> After winning election, Adams became affiliated with the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a>, partly because the National Republican Party's leadership in Massachusetts included many of the former Federalists that Adams had clashed with earlier in his career. The Anti-Masonic Party originated as a movement against <a href="/wiki/Freemasonry" title="Freemasonry">Freemasonry</a>, but it developed into the country's first <a href="/wiki/Third_party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Third party (United States)">third party</a> and embraced a general program of anti-elitism.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams expected a light workload when he returned to Washington at 64 years old, but Speaker <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Stevenson" title="Andrew Stevenson">Andrew Stevenson</a> selected Adams chair of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_Energy_and_Commerce" title="United States House Committee on Energy and Commerce">Committee on Commerce and Manufactures</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Though he identified as a member of the Anti-Masonic Party, Congress was broadly polarized into allies of Jackson and opponents of Jackson, and Adams generally aligned with the latter camp.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Stevenson, an ally of Jackson, expected that the committee chairmanship would keep Adams busy defending the tariff even while the Jacksonian majority on the committee would prevent Adams from accruing any real power.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> As chair of the committee charged with writing tariff laws, Adams became an important player in the <a href="/wiki/Nullification_crisis" title="Nullification crisis">nullification crisis</a>, which stemmed largely from Southern objections to the high rates imposed by the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_Abominations" title="Tariff of Abominations">Tariff of 1828</a>. <a href="/wiki/South_Carolina" title="South Carolina">South Carolina</a> leaders argued that states could <a href="/wiki/Nullification_(U.S._Constitution)" title="Nullification (U.S. Constitution)">nullify federal laws</a>, and they announced that they would bar the federal government from enforcing the tariff in their state.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Adams helped pass the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1832" title="Tariff of 1832">Tariff of 1832</a>, which lowered rates, but not enough to mollify the South Carolina nullifiers. The crisis ended when Clay and Calhoun agreed to another tariff bill, the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1833" title="Tariff of 1833">Tariff of 1833</a>, that furthered lower tariff rates. Adams was appalled by the Nullification Crisis's outcome, as he felt that the Southern states had unfairly benefited from challenging federal law.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> After the crisis, Adams was convinced that Southerners exercised undue influence over the federal government through their control of Jackson's Democratic Party.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1833_Massachusetts_gubernatorial_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1833 Massachusetts gubernatorial election">1833 Massachusetts gubernatorial election</a>, the Anti-Masonic Party nominated Adams in a four-way race between Adams, the National Republican candidate, the Democratic candidate, and a candidate of the Working Men's Party. The National Republican candidate, <a href="/wiki/John_Davis_(Massachusetts_governor)" title="John Davis (Massachusetts governor)">John Davis</a>, won 40% of the vote, while Adams finished in second place with 29%. Because no candidate won a majority of the vote, the state legislature decided the election. Rather than seek election by the legislature, Adams withdrew his name from contention, and the legislature selected Davis.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Adams was nearly elected to the Senate in 1835 by a coalition of Anti-Masons and National Republicans, but his support for Jackson in a minor foreign policy matter annoyed National Republican leaders enough that they dropped their support for his candidacy.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> After 1835, Adams never again sought higher office, focusing instead on his service in the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Van_Buren.2C_Harrison_and_Tyler_administrations.2C_1837.E2.80.931843"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Van_Buren,_Harrison_and_Tyler_administrations,_1837–1843">Van Buren, Harrison and Tyler administrations, 1837–1843</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Hudson,_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_(1844)_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-William_Hudson%2C_Jr._-_Portrait_of_John_Quincy_Adams_%281844%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6392" data-file-height="7977" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Quincy Adams by William Hudson, 1844</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the mid-1830s, the Anti-Masonic Party, the National Republicans, and other groups opposed to Jackson coalesced into the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1836_United_States_presidential_election" title="1836 United States presidential election">1836 presidential election</a> Democrats put forward Martin Van Buren, while the Whigs fielded multiple presidential candidates. Because he disdained all the major party contenders for president, Adams did not take part in the campaign; Van Buren won the election.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, Adams became aligned with the Whig Party in Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Adams generally opposed the initiatives of President Van Buren, long a political adversary, though they maintained a cordial public relationship.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Texas" title="Republic of Texas">Republic of Texas</a> won its independence from Mexico in the <a href="/wiki/Texas_Revolution" title="Texas Revolution">Texas Revolution</a> of 1835–1836. Texas had largely been settled by Americans from the Southern United States, and many of those settlers owned slaves despite an 1829 Mexican law that abolished slavery. Many in the United States and Texas thus favored the admission of Texas into the union as a <a href="/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states" title="Slave states and free states">slave state</a>. Adams considered the issue of Texas to be "a question of far deeper root and more overshadowing branches than any or all others that agitate the country", and he emerged as one of the leading congressional opponents of annexation.  When he served as secretary of state, Adams had sought to acquire Texas, but he argued that, because Mexico had abolished slavery, the acquisition of Texas would transform the region from a free territory into a slave state. He also feared that the annexation of Texas would encourage Southern expansionists to pursue other potential slave states, including <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>. Adams's firm stance may have played a role in discouraging Van Buren from pushing for the annexation of Texas during his presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Whig nominee <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> defeated Van Buren in the <a href="/wiki/1840_United_States_presidential_election" title="1840 United States presidential election">1840 presidential election</a>, and the Whigs gained control of both houses of Congress for the first time. Despite his low regard for Harrison as a person, Adams was enthusiastic about the new Whig administration and the end of the long-standing Democratic dominance of the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> However, Harrison died in April 1841 and was succeeded by Vice President <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a>, a Southerner who, unlike Adams, Henry Clay, and many other prominent Whigs, did not embrace the American System. Adams saw Tyler as an agent of "the slave-driving, Virginia, Jeffersonian school, principled against all improvement". After Tyler vetoed a bill to restore the national bank, Whig congressmen expelled Tyler from the party. Adams was appointed chairman of a special committee that explored impeaching Tyler, and Adams presented a scathing report of Tyler that argued that his actions warranted impeachment. The impeachment process did not move forward, though, because the Whigs did not believe that the Senate would vote to remove Tyler from office.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Opposition_to_the_Mexican-American_War.2C_1844.E2.80.931848"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_to_the_Mexican-American_War,_1844–1848">Opposition to the Mexican-American War, 1844–1848</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg/220px-John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="273" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg/330px-John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg/440px-John_Quincy_Adams_daguerreotype_c1840s.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1612" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>John Quincy Adams, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1840s</span>, Unknown author</figcaption></figure>
<p>Tyler made the annexation of Texas the main foreign policy priority of the later stages of his administration.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> He attempted to win ratification of an annexation treaty in 1844, but, to Adams's surprise and relief, the Senate rejected the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> The annexation of Texas became the central issue of the <a href="/wiki/1844_United_States_presidential_election" title="1844 United States presidential election">1844 presidential election</a>, and Southerners blocked the nomination of Van Buren at the <a href="/wiki/1844_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1844 Democratic National Convention">1844 Democratic National Convention</a> due to the latter's opposition to annexation; the party instead nominated <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>, an acolyte of Andrew Jackson.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Though he once again did not take part in the campaigning, Adams was deeply disappointed that Polk defeated his old ally, Henry Clay, in the 1844 election. He attributed the outcome of the election partly to the <a href="/wiki/Liberty_Party_(United_States,_1840)" title="Liberty Party (United States, 1840)">Liberty Party</a>, a small, <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolitionist</a> third party that may have siphoned votes from Clay in the crucial state of New York.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> After the election, Tyler, whose term would end in March 1845, once again submitted an annexation treaty to Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> Adams strongly attacked the treaty, arguing that the annexation of Texas would involve the United States in "a war for slavery". Despite Adams's opposition, both houses of Congress approved the treaty, with most Democrats voting for annexation and most Whigs voting against it. Texas thus joined the United States as a slave state in 1845.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adams had served with James K. Polk in the House of Representatives, and Adams loathed the new president, seeing him as another expansionist, pro-slavery Southern Democrat.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Adams favored the annexation of the entirety of <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Oregon_boundary_dispute" title="Oregon boundary dispute">disputed region</a> occupied by both the United States and Britain, and was disappointed when President Polk signed the <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Treaty" title="Oregon Treaty">Oregon Treaty</a>, which divided the land between the two claimants at the 49th parallel.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> Polk's expansionist aims centered instead on the Mexican province of <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a>, and he attempted to buy the province from Mexico. The Mexican government refused to sell California or recognize the independence and subsequent American annexation of Texas. Polk deployed a military detachment led by General <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> to back up his assertion that the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Grande" title="Rio Grande">Rio Grande</a> constituted the Southern border of both Texas and the United States. After Taylor's forces clashed with Mexican soldiers north of the Rio Grande, Polk asked for a declaration of war in early 1846, asserting that Mexico had invaded American territory. Though some Whigs questioned whether Mexico had started an aggressive war, both houses of Congress declared war, with the House voting 174-to-14 to approve the declaration. One of the 14 dissenting votes was Adams, who believed that Polk was seeking to wage an offensive to expand slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> After the start of the war, he supported the <a href="/wiki/Wilmot_Proviso" title="Wilmot Proviso">Wilmot Proviso</a>, an unsuccessful legislative proposal that would have banned slavery in any territory ceded by Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> After 1846, ill health increasingly affected Adams, but he continued to oppose the Mexican–American War until his death in 1848.<sup id="cite_ref-Cooper_2017_pages=430–431_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cooper_2017_pages=430–431-204">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Anti-slavery_movement">Anti-slavery movement</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams_and_abolitionism" title="John Quincy Adams and abolitionism">John Quincy Adams and abolitionism</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ADAMS,_John_Q-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/170px-ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="199" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/255px-ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/340px-ADAMS%2C_John_Q-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6902" data-file-height="8066" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing" title="Bureau of Engraving and Printing">BEP</a> engraved portrait of Adams as president</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the 1830s, slavery emerged as an increasingly polarizing issue in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> A longtime opponent of slavery, Adams used his new role in Congress to fight it, and he became the most prominent national leader opposing slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> After one of his reelection victories, he said that he must "bring about a day prophesied when slavery and war shall be banished from the face of the Earth". He wrote in his private journal in 1820:<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The discussion of this <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri question</a> has betrayed the secret of their souls. In the abstract they admit that slavery is an evil, they disclaim it, and cast it all upon the shoulder of Great Britain. But when probed to the quick upon it, they show at the bottom of their souls pride and vainglory in their condition of masterdom. They look down upon the simplicity of a Yankee's manners, because he has no habits of overbearing like theirs and cannot treat negroes like dogs. It is among the evils of slavery that it taints the very sources of moral principle. It establishes false estimates of virtue and vice: for what can be more false and heartless than this doctrine which makes the first and holiest rights of humanity to depend upon the color of the skin?</p></blockquote>
<p>In 1836, partially in response to Adams's consistent presentation of citizen petitions requesting the abolition of slavery in the <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">District of Columbia</a>, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a> imposed a <a href="/wiki/Gag_rule_(United_States)" title="Gag rule (United States)">gag rule</a> that immediately <a href="/wiki/Table_(parliamentary_procedure)" title="Table (parliamentary procedure)">tabled</a> any petitions about slavery. Democrats and Southern Whigs favored the rule, but Northern Whigs, like Adams, opposed it.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> In late 1836, Adams began a campaign to ridicule slave owners and the gag rule. He frequently attempted to present anti-slavery petitions, often in ways that provoked strong reactions from Southern representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Though the gag rule remained in place,<sup id="cite_ref-Miller_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Miller-210">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> the discussion ignited by his actions and the attempts of others to quiet him raised questions of the right to petition, the right to legislative debate, and the morality of slavery. Adams fought actively against the gag rule for another seven years, eventually moving the resolution that led to its repeal in 1844.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1841, at the request of <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Tappan" title="Lewis Tappan">Lewis Tappan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ellis_Gray_Loring" title="Ellis Gray Loring">Ellis Gray Loring</a>, Adams joined the case of <i><a href="/wiki/United_States_v._The_Amistad" title="United States v. The Amistad">United States v. The Amistad</a></i>. Adams went before the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court</a> on behalf of African slaves who had revolted and seized the Spanish ship <i>Amistad</i>. Adams appeared on February 24, 1841, and spoke for four hours. His argument succeeded: the Court ruled that the Africans were free and they returned to their homes.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Smithsonian_Institution">Smithsonian Institution</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-George_Caleb_Bingham_-_John_Quincy_Adams_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4728" data-file-height="5641" /></a><figcaption>Adams's portrait at the <a href="/wiki/National_Portrait_Gallery_(United_States)" title="National Portrait Gallery (United States)">U.S. National Portrait Gallery</a> by <a href="/wiki/George_Caleb_Bingham" title="George Caleb Bingham">George Bingham</a> c. 1850 copy of an 1844 original</figcaption></figure>
<p>Adams also became a leading force for the promotion of science. In 1829, British scientist <a href="/wiki/James_Smithson" title="James Smithson">James Smithson</a> died, and he left his fortune for the "increase and diffusion of knowledge". In Smithson's will, he stated that should his nephew, Henry James Hungerford, die without heirs, the Smithson estate would go to the government of the United States to create an "Establishment for the increase and diffusion of Knowledge among men". After the nephew died without heirs in 1835, President Andrew Jackson informed <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">Congress</a> of the bequest, which amounted to about US$500,000 (US$75 million in 2008 dollars after inflation). Adams realized that this might allow the United States to realize his dream of building a national institution of science and learning. Adams thus became Congress's primary supporter of the future <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The money was invested in shaky state <a href="/wiki/Bond_(finance)" title="Bond (finance)">bonds</a>, which quickly defaulted. After heated debate in Congress, Adams successfully argued to restore the lost funds with interest.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Though Congress wanted to use the money for other purposes, Adams successfully persuaded Congress to preserve the money for an institution of science and learning. Congress also debated whether the federal government had the authority to accept the gift, though with Adams leading the initiative, Congress accepted the legacy bequeathed to the nation and pledged the faith of the United States to the charitable trust on July 1, 1836.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Partly due to Adams's efforts, Congress voted to establish the <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a> in 1846. A nonpolitical board of regents was established to lead the institution, which included a museum, art gallery, library, and laboratory.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG/180px-JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG" decoding="async" width="180" height="135" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG/270px-JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG/360px-JQAdams_QRc_CC.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3901" data-file-height="2925" /></a><figcaption>Adams's <a href="/wiki/Cenotaph" title="Cenotaph">cenotaph</a> at the Congressional Cemetery</figcaption></figure>
<p>In mid-November 1846, the 78-year-old former president suffered a stroke that left him partially paralyzed. After a few months of rest, he made a full recovery and resumed his duties in Congress. When Adams entered the House chamber on February 13, 1847, everyone "stood up and applauded".<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg/220px-John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg/330px-John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg/440px-John_Quincy_Adams_drawing2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3945" data-file-height="3236" /></a><figcaption>John Quincy Adams during his final hours of life after his collapse in the Capitol. Drawing in pencil by Arthur Joseph Stansbury, digitally restored.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On February 21, 1848, the House of Representatives was discussing the matter of honoring <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army" title="United States Army">United States Army</a> officers who served in the Mexican–American War. Adams had been a vehement critic of the war, and as Congressmen rose up to say, "Aye!" in favor of the measure, he instead yelled, "No!"<sup id="cite_ref-parker-1848_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-parker-1848-218">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> He rose to answer a question put forth by Speaker of the House <a href="/wiki/Robert_Charles_Winthrop" title="Robert Charles Winthrop">Robert Charles Winthrop</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-donaldson_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-donaldson-219">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Immediately thereafter, Adams collapsed, having suffered a massive <a href="/wiki/Cerebral_hemorrhage" class="mw-redirect" title="Cerebral hemorrhage">cerebral hemorrhage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-widmer-2008_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-widmer-2008-220">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Two days later, on February 23, he died at 7:20&#160;p.m. with his wife at his side in the Speaker's Room inside the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol" title="United States Capitol">Capitol Building</a> in Washington, D.C.; his only living child, Charles Francis, did not arrive in time to see his father alive. His last words were "This is the last of Earth. I am content".<sup id="cite_ref-donaldson_219-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-donaldson-219">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Among those present for his death was <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>, then a freshman representative from Illinois.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JQ_Adams_Vault,_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/8/82/JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg/220px-JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/8/82/JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg/330px-JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/8/82/JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg/440px-JQ_Adams_Vault%2C_Hancock_Cemetery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>John Quincy Adams's original tomb at <a href="/wiki/Hancock_Cemetery" title="Hancock Cemetery">Hancock Cemetery</a>, across the street from <a href="/wiki/United_First_Parish_Church" title="United First Parish Church">United First Parish Church</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>His original interment was temporary, in the public vault at the <a href="/wiki/Congressional_Cemetery" title="Congressional Cemetery">Congressional Cemetery</a> in Washington, D.C. Later, he was interred in the family burial ground in <a href="/wiki/Quincy,_Massachusetts" title="Quincy, Massachusetts">Quincy, Massachusetts</a>, across from the (<a href="/wiki/Unitarianism" title="Unitarianism">Unitarian</a>) <a href="/wiki/United_First_Parish_Church" title="United First Parish Church">United First Parish Church</a>, called <a href="/wiki/Hancock_Cemetery" title="Hancock Cemetery">Hancock Cemetery</a>. After Louisa's death in 1852, his son had his parents re-interred in the expanded family crypt in the United First Parish Church across the street, next to John and Abigail. Both tombs are viewable by the public. Adams's original tomb at Hancock Cemetery is still there and marked simply "J.Q. Adams".<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Adams_political_family" title="Adams political family">Adams political family</a></div>
<p>Adams and Louisa had three sons and a daughter. Their daughter, Louisa, was born in 1811 but died in 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> They named their first son <a href="/wiki/George_Washington_Adams" title="George Washington Adams">George Washington Adams</a> (1801–1829) after the first president. This decision upset Adams's mother, and, by her account, his father as well.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Both George and their second son, <a href="/wiki/John_Adams_II" title="John Adams II">John</a> (1803–1834), led troubled lives and died in early adulthood.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> George, who had long suffered from <a href="/wiki/Alcoholism" title="Alcoholism">alcoholism</a>, died in 1829 after going overboard on a steamboat; it is not clear whether he fell or purposely jumped from the boat.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> John, who ran an unprofitable flour and grist mill owned by his father, died of an unknown illness in 1834.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Adams's youngest son, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Francis_Adams_Sr." title="Charles Francis Adams Sr.">Charles Francis Adams Sr.</a>, was an important leader of the "Conscience Whigs", a Northern, anti-slavery faction of the Whig Party.<sup id="cite_ref-Cooper_2017_pages=430–431_204-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cooper_2017_pages=430–431-204">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Charles served as the <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a>'s vice presidential candidate in the <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 presidential election</a> and later became a prominent member of the <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican Party</a>, serving as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Minister_to_England" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Minister to England">United States Minister to England</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality">Personality</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_$1_Coin_obverse.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg/170px-John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg/255px-John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg/340px-John_Quincy_Adams_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1098" data-file-height="1098" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Presidential_Dollar" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidential Dollar">Presidential Dollar</a> of John Quincy Adams</figcaption></figure>
<p>Adams's personality and political beliefs were much like his father's.<sup id="cite_ref-Nagel_1999_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nagel_1999-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> He always preferred solitary reading to social engagements, and he was repeatedly persuaded to stay in public service by others. Historian Paul Nagel states that, like <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> after him, Adams often suffered from depression, for which he sought treatment in early years. Adams thought his depression was due to the high expectations demanded of him by his father and mother. Throughout his life, he felt inadequate and socially awkward because of his depression, and was constantly bothered by his physical appearance.<sup id="cite_ref-Nagel_1999_230-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nagel_1999-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> He was closer to his father, with whom he spent much of his early life while abroad, than he was to his mother. In his youth, while the American Revolution raged on, Adams heard from his mother about his father's work and the substantial risks he took to support it. As a result, he developed a deep respect for his father.<sup id="cite_ref-Nagel_1999_230-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nagel_1999-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, Adams had a rocky relationship with his mother, due to her high expectations of him, and her fear that her children would follow in the footsteps of her brother, who died of alcoholism.<sup id="cite_ref-Nagel_1999_230-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nagel_1999-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> His biographer, Nagel, concludes that his mother's disapproval of Louisa Johnson motivated him to marry Johnson in 1797, despite Adams's reservations that Johnson, like his mother, had a forceful personality.<sup id="cite_ref-Nagel_1999_230-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nagel_1999-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though Adams wore a <a href="/wiki/Wig#19th_and_20th_centuries" title="Wig">powdered wig tied in a queue</a> in his youth, he abandoned this fashion while serving as the <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_Russia" title="List of ambassadors of the United States to Russia">U.S. Minister to Russia</a> (1809-1814)<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_firsts#John_Quincy_Adams_(1825–1829)" title="List of United States presidential firsts">became the first president</a> <a href="/wiki/1795%E2%80%931820_in_Western_fashion#Men&#39;s_fashion" title="1795–1820 in Western fashion">to adopt a short haircut</a> instead of long hair tied in a <a href="/wiki/Queue_(hairstyle)" title="Queue (hairstyle)">queue</a> and to regularly wear long <a href="/wiki/Trousers" title="Trousers">trousers</a> instead of <a href="/wiki/Culottes" title="Culottes">knee breeches</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/1820s_in_Western_fashion#Men&#39;s_wear" title="1820s in Western fashion">fashion of the 19th century</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> It has been suggested that John Quincy Adams had the highest <a href="/wiki/Intelligence_quotient" title="Intelligence quotient">I.Q.</a> of any U.S. president.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dean_Simonton" title="Dean Simonton">Dean Simonton</a>, a professor of psychology at <a href="/wiki/University_of_California,_Davis" title="University of California, Davis">UC Davis</a>, estimated his <a href="/wiki/Intelligence_quotient" title="Intelligence quotient">I.Q.</a> score at 165.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> He reportedly spoke eight foreign languages (Dutch, French, German, Greek, Italian, Latin, Russian, and Spanish), more than any other U.S. president. He remains the only U.S. president who could converse in Russian.<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy,_1858.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg/220px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg/330px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg/440px-John_Quincy_Adams_by_GPA_Healy%2C_1858.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2284" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Official portrait of Adams by <a href="/wiki/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy" title="George Peter Alexander Healy">George Peter Alexander Healy</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1858</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Adams is widely regarded as one of the most effective diplomats and secretaries of state in American history,<sup id="cite_ref-cooperpref_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooperpref-238">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> but <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">scholars generally rank</a> him as an average president.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Adams is remembered as a man eminently qualified for the presidency, yet hopelessly weakened in his presidential leadership potential because of the 1824 election. Most importantly, Adams is remembered as a poor politician in an era when politics had begun to matter more. He spoke of trying to serve as a man above the "baneful weed of party strife" at the precise moment in history when the <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a> was emerging with nearly revolutionary force.<sup id="cite_ref-millerleg_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-millerleg-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Biographer and historian <a href="/wiki/William_J._Cooper_Jr." title="William J. Cooper Jr.">William J. Cooper Jr.</a> comments that Adams "does not loom large in the American imagination", but that he has received more public attention since the late 20th century due to his anti-slavery stances. Cooper writes that Adams was the first "major public figure" to publicly question whether the United States could remain united so long as the institution of slavery persisted.<sup id="cite_ref-cooperpref_238-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooperpref-238">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Historian Daniel Walker Howe writes that Adams's "intellectual ability and courage were above reproach, and his wisdom in perceiving the national interest has stood the test of time".<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> He has also been praised as a strong prose stylist, with James Parker describing him as one of the "three authentically muddy-eyed and pained-by-subjectivity <i>writers</i>" that the White House has harbored, along with <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> and <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Peacefield - John Adams&#39;s Home" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg/220px-John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg/330px-John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg/440px-John_Adams%27_Peacefield.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3872" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption>Peacefield - John Quincy Adams's Home</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams_Birthplace" title="John Quincy Adams Birthplace">John Quincy Adams Birthplace</a> is now part of <a href="/wiki/Adams_National_Historical_Park" title="Adams National Historical Park">Adams National Historical Park</a> and open to the public. <a href="/wiki/Adams_House_(Harvard_University)" class="mw-redirect" title="Adams House (Harvard University)">Adams House</a>, one of twelve undergraduate residential Houses at Harvard University, is named for John Adams, John Quincy Adams, and other members of the Adams family associated with Harvard.<sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> John Quincy Adams tower, located in the Southwest residential area of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Massachusetts_Amherst" title="University of Massachusetts Amherst">University of Massachusetts Amherst</a>, is named for the president.<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> In 1870, Charles Francis built the first <a href="/wiki/Presidential_library_system" title="Presidential library system">presidential library</a> in the United States, to honor his father. The <a href="/wiki/Adams_National_Historical_Park#Stone_Library" title="Adams National Historical Park">Stone Library</a> includes over 14,000 books written in twelve languages. The library is located at <a href="/wiki/Peacefield" title="Peacefield">Peacefield</a> (the "Old House") at <a href="/wiki/Adams_National_Historical_Park" title="Adams National Historical Park">Adams National Historical Park</a> in <a href="/wiki/Quincy,_Massachusetts" title="Quincy, Massachusetts">Quincy, Massachusetts</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Graves_of_the_Adams,_Quincy,_Massachusetts.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG/220px-Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG/330px-Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG/440px-Graves_of_the_Adams%2C_Quincy%2C_Massachusetts.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption>Tombs of Presidents <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a> (far left) and John Quincy Adams (right) and their wives Abigail and Louisa, in a family crypt beneath the <a href="/wiki/United_First_Parish_Church,_Quincy,_Massachusetts" class="mw-redirect" title="United First Parish Church, Quincy, Massachusetts">United First Parish Church</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>An <a href="/wiki/Adams_Memorial" title="Adams Memorial">Adams Memorial</a> has been proposed in Washington, D.C., honoring Adams and his wife, son, father, mother, and other members of their family.
</p><p>Adams's middle name of Quincy has been used by several locations in the United States, including the town of <a href="/wiki/Quincy,_Illinois" title="Quincy, Illinois">Quincy, Illinois</a>. <a href="/wiki/Adams_County,_Illinois" title="Adams County, Illinois">Adams County, Illinois</a> and <a href="/wiki/Adams_County,_Indiana" title="Adams County, Indiana">Adams County, Indiana</a> are also named after Adams. <a href="/wiki/Adams_County,_Iowa" title="Adams County, Iowa">Adams County, Iowa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Adams_County,_Wisconsin" title="Adams County, Wisconsin">Adams County, Wisconsin</a>, were each named for either John Adams or John Quincy Adams.
</p><p>Some sources contend that in 1843 Adams sat for the earliest confirmed photograph of a United States president, although others maintain that <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> had posed even earlier for his portrait, in 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-whha-16_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whha-16-246">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> The original <a href="/wiki/Daguerreotype" title="Daguerreotype">daguerreotype</a> is in the collection of the <a href="/wiki/National_Portrait_Gallery_(United_States)" title="National Portrait Gallery (United States)">National Portrait Gallery</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Film_and_television">Film and television</span></h3>
<p>Adams occasionally is featured in the <a href="/wiki/Mass_media" title="Mass media">mass media</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/PBS" title="PBS">PBS</a> miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/The_Adams_Chronicles" title="The Adams Chronicles">The Adams Chronicles</a></i> (1976), he was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/David_Birney" title="David Birney">David Birney</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Daniels" title="William Daniels">William Daniels</a>, Marcel Trenchard, Steven Grover and Mark Winkworth. He was also portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Hopkins" title="Anthony Hopkins">Anthony Hopkins</a> in the 1997 film <i><a href="/wiki/Amistad_(film)" title="Amistad (film)">Amistad</a></i>, and again by <a href="/wiki/Ebon_Moss-Bachrach" title="Ebon Moss-Bachrach">Ebon Moss-Bachrach</a> and Steven Hinkle in the 2008 <a href="/wiki/HBO" title="HBO">HBO</a> television miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/John_Adams_(TV_miniseries)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Adams (TV miniseries)">John Adams</a></i>.
</p>
